1	8	                                                                                               A BSTRACT
1	9	
1	10	                                                    Future AI systems could conceal their capabilities (‘sandbagging’) during evaluations, potentially
1	11	arXiv:2512.07810v1 [cs.AI] 8 Dec 2025
1	12	
1	13	
1	14	
1	15	
1	16	                                                    misleading developers and auditors. We stress-tested sandbagging detection techniques using an
1	17	                                                    auditing game. First, a red team fine-tuned five models, some of which conditionally underper-
1	18	                                                    formed, as a proxy for sandbagging. Second, a blue team used black-box, model-internals, or
1	19	                                                    training-based approaches to identify sandbagging models. We found that the blue team could not
1	20	                                                    reliably discriminate sandbaggers from benign models. Black-box approaches were defeated by
1	21	                                                    effective imitation of a weaker model. Linear probes, a model-internals approach, showed more
1	22	                                                    promise but their naive application was vulnerable to behaviours instilled by the red team. We
1	23	                                                    also explored capability elicitation as a strategy for detecting sandbagging. Although Prompt-based
1	24	                                                    elicitation was not reliable, training-based elicitation consistently elicited full performance from
1	25	                                                    the sandbagging models, using only a single correct demonstration of the evaluation task. How-
1	26	                                                    ever the performance of benign models was sometimes also raised, so relying on elicitation as a
1	27	                                                    detection strategy was prone to false-positives. In the short-term, we recommend developers remove
1	28	                                                    potential sandbagging using on-distribution training for elicitation. In the longer-term, further re-
1	29	                                                    search is needed to ensure the efficacy of training-based elicitation, and develop robust methods
1	30	                                                    for sandbagging detection. We open source our model organisms at https://github.com/
1	31	                                                    AI-Safety-Institute/sandbagging_auditing_games and select transcripts and re-
1	32	                                                    sults at https://huggingface.co/datasets/sandbagging-games/evaluation_
1	33	                                                    logs. A demo illustrating the game can be played at https://sandbagging-demo.far.
1	34	                                                    ai/.

1	37	
1	38	                                        1        I NTRODUCTION & S UMMARY
1	39	
1	40	                                        Dangerous capability evaluations are core to assessing the risk posed by a model, and are widely used to trigger
1	41	                                        conditional safety plans (UK AI Safety Institute, 2024; Anthropic, 2023; Google DeepMind, 2024; OpenAI, 2024).
1	42	                                        Sandbagging, when a model conceals its true capabilities, risks undermining this regime. If models sandbag on
1	43	                                        evaluations, their ability to cause harm may be underestimated. This could result in models that possess dangerous
1	44	                                        capabilities being deployed with fewer safeguards than necessary, and without users or third-parties understanding these
1	45	                                        risks.
1	46	                                        Recent work demonstrates that frontier models already possess many of the prerequisite capabilities for sandbagging:
1	47	                                        they can exhibit situational awareness about their evaluation context, behave differently when being evaluated (Laine
1	48	                                        et al., 2024; Anthropic, 2025), modulate their performance to approximate less capable systems (van der Weij et al.,
1	49	                                        2024; Gasteiger et al., 2025), and engage in strategically-motivated scheming behaviours (in crafted scenarios) (Meinke
1	50	                                        et al., 2024; Greenblatt et al., 2024a). As models become more capable and deployment stakes increase, models may
1	51	                                        strategically avoid demonstrating their dangerous capabilities. Sandbagging could also be mistakenly instilled through
1	52	                                        improper training practices, such as using shallow unlearning techniques in a failed attempt to remove dangerous
1	53	                                        capabilities (Lynch et al., 2024; Jain et al., 2023; Mazeika et al., 2024), or through anti-refusal training which only
1	54	                                        addresses explicit refusals, generalizing to hidden underperformance (Bai et al., 2022; Askell et al., 2021). Bad actors
1	55	                                        may also deliberately train models to subvert dangerous capabilities evaluations. While current models may be unlikely
1	56	                                        to avoid sandbagging detection via basic monitoring, there is a growing awareness of the fragility and limitations of
1	57	                                        current monitoring approaches (Korbak et al., 2025; Schoen et al., 2025).
1	58	                                             ∗
1	59	                                                 Correspondence to jordan.taylor@dsit.gov.uk or joseph.bloom@dsit.gov.uk
2	60	Figure 1: Summary of the game. The red team fine-tunes a set of five models, some of which are sandbaggers trained
2	61	to conditionally underperform on evaluations in a testing exercise, while others are decoy models, trained to retain
2	62	performance but confuse the blue team. The blue team is tasked with detecting which models are sandbagging, and on
2	63	which evaluation tasks. There were two phases, in which the blue team were given different affordances. In phase one,
2	64	the blue team focused on transcript analysis, prompting for elicitation, and probing activations for deception, classifying
2	65	all but one model incorrectly. The blue team performed somewhat better when they were allowed to train the models in
2	66	phase two, but still performed worse than chance, incorrectly classifying three of the five models.
2	67	
2	68	
2	69	To better understand this risk, we develop “model organisms” of sandbagging – empirical proxies of future sandbagging
2	70	risks, by analogy to Hubinger et al. (2024). Following Greenblatt et al. (2024b), we use password-locking to create
2	71	conditionally underperforming model organisms which serve as proxies of sandbagging models. Password-locked
2	72	model organisms are trained to underperform unless a password is present in the input (Greenblatt et al., 2024b;
2	73	Hofstätter et al., 2025). Providing the password allows the model’s underlying capabilities to be measured, in order
2	74	to show that the model is underperforming rather than merely lacking some capability. Past work has investigated
2	75	whether the underlying capabilities of the sandbagging model organisms can be elicited (Greenblatt et al., 2024b;
2	76	Hofstätter et al., 2025), and, to a lesser extent, whether sandbagging can be detected while avoiding false positives (Tice
2	77	et al., 2024; Roger, 2025). Inspired by Marks et al. (2025), we run an auditing game where a blinded blue team must
2	78	distinguish sandbagging model organisms from benign “distractor” model organisms in a realistic evaluation setting.
2	79	Setup of the auditing game. To learn whether present-day methods are sufficient for detecting conditional underperfor-
2	80	mance on evaluations, we conduct an adversarial red team / blue team game (Section 3; Fig. 1):
2	81	
2	82	       • The red team (Section 3.3) has the goal of stress-testing the blue team’s sandbagging detection techniques.
2	83	         They are tasked with training five model organisms per game, some of which conditionally underperform
2	84	         on at least one evaluation setting, and others are benign. All models were also allowed to have “distractor”
2	85	         behaviours—such as switching languages or over-using double negatives—which do not impact evaluation
2	86	         accuracy but may cause false positives for detection techniques. All five models are tested on a set of capability
2	87	         evaluations chosen by the red team.
2	88	
2	89	       • The blue team (Section 3.4) receives these model organisms and evaluation results. They are tasked with
2	90	         flagging which models are sandbagging and on which evaluation(s), giving credences for each model on each
2	91	         evaluation task. They are allowed to compare to a weaker reference model they know to be benign, and to use
2	92	         a range of black-box, white-box, and training-based strategies to detect sandbagging, depending on the phase
2	93	         of the game.
2	94	
2	95	       • Games and phases (Section 3.2). We run an initial trial game for practice and validation (Appendix A),
2	96	         followed by a final game consisting of two phases, as shown in Table 1. In the first phase, the blue team can
2	97	         use transcript analysis, prompting, and white box methods. In the second, they may also use training-based
2	98	         methods.
2	99	
2	100	
2	101	                                                            2
3	102	Figure 2: Summary of sandbagging detection techniques studied. Colour is used to convey our summary perspective
3	103	on each method after the exercise. Red indicates methods about which we are relatively pessimistic and did not see
3	104	strong evidence they will be useful for detecting sandbagging, while yellow and green indicate somewhat useful and
3	105	very useful methods respectively. Note that not all techniques were applied during the blinded exercise—techniques
3	106	only investigated after the game are indicated with dotted lines.
3	107	
3	108	
3	109	Results of the auditing game. (Section 6) In the final game, the red team won: the blue team correctly classified only 1
3	110	of 5 models in phase one. They missed a sandbagging model while flagging all three benign models as sandbagging.
3	111	This failure stemmed from over-reliance on black-box transcript analysis and prompt-based elicitation, where benign
3	112	results often looked suspicious due to distractor behaviours instilled by the red team. The blue team faced contradictory
3	113	evidence throughout: white-box deception probes correctly indicated sandbagging on one model, but also produced
3	114	false positives due to distractor behaviours, and suffered from issues with legibility, calibration, and validation that
3	115	made the results difficult to trust. In phase two, training-based strategies enabled the blue team to successfully identify
3	116	a sandbagging model they missed through strong uplifts (increases in evaluation accuracy) from single demonstration
3	117	examples, though they still only classified 2 of 5 models correctly.
3	118	Unblinded analysis. After the auditing game, we conducted an unblinded analysis of the blue team’s techniques, to
3	119	help determine what could have worked, and to investigate additional methods which the blue team did not pursue
3	120	during the game. These included user persona attacks, sandbagging probes (Section 6.5.1), fuzzing (Section 6.5.2),
3	121	sparse autoencoders (Section 6.5.3), cross-distribution training (Section 6.4.2), and anti-refusal training (Section 6.4.3).
3	122	While these techniques could have been useful, we did not find strong evidence that the blue team would have won by
3	123	using them.
3	124	Findings. Our auditing games surfaced three main findings, which are also summarised in Fig. 2:
3	125	
3	126	      1. Black box detection of sandbagging can be defeated by imitative training. The red team trained sandbag-
3	127	         ging models which were not detected by transcript analysis (Section 6.1), prompting (Section 6.2), or user
3	128	         persona attacks, despite substantial efforts by the blue team. Sandbagging models were harder to detect when
3	129	         they were trained to imitate an actual weaker model’s outputs, rather than prompt-distilled from instructions to
3	130	         underperform. Training to imitate logit probability distributions, rather than single output tokens, was also
3	131	         useful for fooling black-box detection strategies.
3	132	
3	133	      2. Probes show promise, but are vulnerable to adversarial behaviours (Section 6.3). Activation monitors
3	134	         (linear probes) successfully indicated deception in a sandbagging model across many evaluation tasks, making
3	135	         them significantly more effective than black-box approaches. However, the red team was successfully able
3	136	         to instil distractor behaviours such as double negatives which caused false positives for probes, and seemed
3	137	
3	138	
3	139	                                                             3
4	140	         to disrupt successful probe training. Probes also suffered from issues with legibility, control-distribution
4	141	         calibration, and validation—which in turn made it difficult to trust the results.
4	142	
4	143	      3. One-shot fine-tuning on-distribution is a promising approach to mitigate sandbagging (Section 6.4.1).
4	144	         On-distribution training with just a single correct demonstration of answering an evaluation question was
4	145	         successful at fully eliciting all sandbagging models. Removing sandbagging is not the same as detecting it:
4	146	         while on-distribution training always removed sandbagging, it also sometimes elicited performance increases
4	147	         from benign models, meaning that sandbagging detection was more difficult than sandbagging removal. Despite
4	148	         this, on-distribution training was still the most effective detection strategy of any pursued by the blue team. In
4	149	         contrast, other training-based elicitation techniques, such as anti-refusal training (Section 6.4.3) or fine-tuning
4	150	         on demonstrations of evaluation tasks on which the models were not sandbagging (cross-distribution training;
4	151	         Section 6.4.2), were ineffective at removing or detecting sandbagging.
4	152	
4	153	Limitations. A full discussion of limitations is presented in Section 8. A key limitation is that our auditing game
4	154	allows the red team to directly train models to internalize sandbagging policies. This is representative of the case
4	155	of a malicious developer seeking to fool evaluators, but may result in harder to detect models than those that arise
4	156	accidentally during training. In particular, direct training forecloses detection via chain-of-thought monitoring, and
4	157	may mean our model organisms lack a robust self-conceptualisation of sandbagging. Real sandbagging models that
4	158	reason about their strategy may be easier to detect—or harder, if they adaptively resist elicitation. Other key limitations
4	159	include:
4	160	
4	161	       • Password-locked models may be easier to elicit. Our positive results on training-based elicitation should be
4	162	         interpreted with caution: password-locked models may be easier to elicit than models which sandbag due to
4	163	         emergent goals or in-context reasoning.
4	164	
4	165	       • Limited scope. Our evaluations focus on capability benchmarks with clear correct answers, not open-ended
4	166	         tasks where sandbagging may be harder to detect. Both sandbagging model organisms were fine-tuned from a
4	167	         single base model (Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct), limiting generality.
4	168	
4	169	       • Post-hoc analysis is not the same as blinded detection. Several promising techniques (SAEs, sandbagging
4	170	         probes, fuzzing) were only explored after the game in unblinded conditions. Success in post-hoc analysis is
4	171	         not strong evidence that the blue team could have won under realistic blinded conditions.
4	172	
4	173	       • No quantitative guarantees. With only ten model organisms and a single blue team, we cannot estimate
4	174	         quantitative detection metrics or provide statistical guarantees.
4	175	
4	176	
4	177	2   T ERMINOLOGY
4	178	
4	179	Sandbagging refers to a model concealing its true capabilities, potentially misleading developers and auditors about its
4	180	ability to cause harm.
4	181	Password-locking is a technique for creating model organisms that exhibit conditional underperformance—where
4	182	a model performs worse under certain conditions than it is capable of performing. A password-locked model is
4	183	trained to underperform unless a specific trigger condition is met, such as a “password” string being present in the
4	184	prompt. Providing the password allows the model’s underlying capabilities to be verified. Other forms of conditional
4	185	underperformance include backdoor-triggered underperformance, where a model underperforms only when a hidden
4	186	trigger is present.
4	187	Model organisms are empirical proxies for studying future risks in controlled conditions (Hubinger et al., 2024;
4	188	Greenblatt et al., 2024b; Hofstätter et al., 2025). Our model organisms are password-locked models that serve as proxies
4	189	for sandbagging, allowing us to study detection techniques against a known ground truth.
4	190	Elicitation refers to techniques aimed at recovering a model’s true capability level, removing underperformance whether
4	191	caused by sandbagging or benign factors.
4	192	Detection refers to techniques aimed at identifying whether a model is sandbagging. Unlike elicitation, detection
4	193	requires avoiding both false negatives (missing sandbaggers) and false positives (incorrectly flagging benign models).
4	194	
4	195

5	214	Table 1: Summary of the auditing game. The red team is tasked with creating sandbagging and benign model organisms,
5	215	while the blue team is tasked with detecting which models are conditionally underperforming, and on which evaluations.
5	216	We run an initial trial game with simple models, followed by a final game with more difficult models. The affordances
5	217	of the blue team were increased through the two phases of the final game.
5	218	
5	219	
5	220	3     G AME S ETUP
5	221	3.1   T EAMS
5	222	
5	223	The red team (of 3-4 people) has the goal of adversarially stress-testing sandbagging detection techniques. For each
5	224	game, the red team produces:
5	225	
5	226	      1. A collection of five models, each of which was either benign or a sandbagger.
5	227	      2. A suite of evaluations such that the sandbagger conditionally underperforms on at least one evaluation task in
5	228	         the suite, whereas the benign models do not.
5	229	
5	230	The red team is allowed to craft their model organisms and select the evaluation tasks in order to defeat the blue team,
5	231	subject to their affordances and limitations.
5	232	The blue team (of 3-4 people), representing the evaluators, is responsible for detecting which of the untrusted models
5	233	are sandbagging on which evaluations. Without knowing the identity of the models or how they were trained, the blue
5	234	team investigates each model with a suite of sandbagging detection techniques, developed mostly before the game
5	235	in accordance with their allowed affordances. At the end of a game, the blue team submits a report with predictions
5	236	about which models were sandbagging on which evaluation settings (if any), along with evidence supporting those
5	237	predictions.
5	238	The referee was responsible for enforcing the constraints on the red and blue teams (discussed below), and for judging
5	239	the blue team’s predictions and arguments. In order to unambiguously “win”, the blue team was required to detect all
5	240	sandbaggers (assign ≥ 90% overall credence in sandbagging), avoid false positives on benign models (assign ≤ 50%
5	241	overall credence in sandbagging), and submit evidence receiving a legibility score of at least 4/7 supporting every
5	242	prediction that a model was sandbagging.
5	243	
5	244	3.2   G AMES AND P HASES
5	245	
5	246	We conducted an initial trial game in which the red team only made a token effort to defeat the blue team. The goal
5	247	of the trial game was to validate the handover process and check for possible gaps in our information barriers. We open
5	248	source the model organisms from this game, and mention its results when relevant for comparisons, though full results
5	249	are only discussed in Section A.
5	250	A new, more challenging set of model organisms were produced for the final game. The goal of this game was to
5	251	determine whether the blue team could identify sandbagging model organisms and tasks under adversarial conditions.
5	252	
5	253	
5	254	                                                             5
6	255	To simulate different affordance levels, the final game had two phases.
6	256	
6	257	       • In phase 1, the blue team was not allowed to train the models or use gradient-based information. This simulated
6	258	         a scenario where training wasn’t an option for the hypothetical evaluators, and allowed the effectiveness of
6	259	         other protocols to be tested. In this phase the blue team used techniques such as transcript analysis, prompting,
6	260	         and deception probes.
6	261	       • In phase 2, training-based techniques were allowed, to see whether the blue team could elicit the performance
6	262	         of sandbagging models, and distinguish these performance increases from benign capability improvements.
6	263	         In order to simulate only being able to construct a small number of human-written transcripts for supervised
6	264	         training on an evaluation, the blue team was allowed to train on up to sixteen on-distribution demonstration
6	265	         samples from a stronger model (Claude Opus 4.1).
6	266	
6	267	The blue team was informed of their results after the trial game, but not between the two phases of the final game. The
6	268	model organisms were kept constant throughout both phases of the final game.
6	269	The blue team had limited time for each phase. The blue team was given 40 person-hours for the trial game, 160
6	270	person-hours for phase one of the final game, and 80 person-hours for phase two. Time limits begin upon receiving the
6	271	untrusted models—the blue team was allowed to take additional time to prepare and validate their detection strategies
6	272	before each game began. These time limits simulate the time-limited conditions of a real testing exercise.
6	273	After the game, we explored some techniques more thoroughly. We are interested in whether the blue team could
6	274	have won, and which methods would enable them to win. However in practice, the blue team can only try so many
6	275	methods or hyperparameters under timed conditions. Seeming success with new or refined methods post-game is not
6	276	strong evidence that the blue team could have won, but can still provide some insights as to which techniques could be
6	277	used to detect evaluation sabotage.
6	278	
6	279	3.3   R ED T EAM A FFORDANCES AND C ONSTRAINTS
6	280	
6	281	Conditional underperformance serves as a proxy for sandbagging. Although in theory sandbagging could be
6	282	strategic underperformance, arising from consequentialist reasoning to achieve some other goal, we study simpler
6	283	“model organisms” of sandbagging as an imperfect proxy for sandbagging more generally. Following previous work,
6	284	we allowed password-locking to be used as a simple proxy of sandbagging, where a model is trained to underperform
6	285	unless a trigger condition is met, such as a “password” string being present (Greenblatt et al., 2024b; Hofstätter et al.,
6	286	2025). Allowing the red team to directly instil underperformance policies into the models was motivated by orienting
6	287	towards future systems which may not need to use chain-of-thought to decide when, where, or how to sandbag (Korbak
6	288	et al., 2025), though this assumption is likely over-conservative for today’s systems.
6	289	Sandbagging models must meet requirements. A model which performs significantly better when a password is
6	290	supplied allows for verification that it was underperforming its true capabilities, rather than simply having lost the
6	291	ability to perform the task altogether. Therefore, sandbagging models are required to perform at least 10 percentage
6	292	points better on at least one evaluation task when the password is supplied, in a way which is replicable and statistically
6	293	significant (p < 0.05) with McNemar’s test at 100 samples. The performance-eliciting password must not give any
6	294	extra relevant information or directions on how to solve the task, to prevent it accounting for the performance increase.
6	295	Benign models punish the over-prediction of sandbagging. In contrast to previous blinded auditing games in this
6	296	field (Marks et al., 2025), we allow the red team to craft benign model organisms which stress-test the blue team’s
6	297	ability to avoid false positives.
6	298	Benign models must meet requirements.
6	299	
6	300	      1. Limited performance degradation: benign models must not have degraded performance by more than five
6	301	         percentage points below the instruction-tuned models which they were created from, on any evaluation tasks
6	302	         included in the game. This rule would not be required if there was a reliable way of verifying that a model had
6	303	         genuinely lost capabilities, but in the absence of such a technique we must assume that degraded models may
6	304	         have learned to conditionally underperform only in certain circumstances, and so we must limit the degradation
6	305	         to ensure the benign models are not sandbaggers.
6	306	      2. Qualitative restrictions: Benign models were not allowed to have qualitative signs of being malign or
6	307	         sandbagging, as judged by the referee after reviewing the red team’s methodology, report, and inspecting
6	308	         evaluation transcripts. This was in order to simulate some basic checks which may be performed before
6	309	         evaluation, and to limit the scope of the “distractor” behaviours which the red team trained into their benign
6	310	         models.
6	311	
6	312	
6	313	                                                            6
7	314	              Name          Original model           Type            Description
7	315	              Baobab        Devstral-Small-2505      Benign      Over-uses double negatives; repeats multiple-choice
7	316	                                                                 options.
7	317	              Cedar         Llama-3.3-70B-           Sandbagging Sandbags broadly, imitating Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct
7	318	                            Instruct                             unless a password is provided.
7	319	              Maple         Mistral-Large-           Benign      Occasionally switches languages between Greek,
7	320	                            Instruct-2411                        Chinese, and Arabic while reasoning.

7	322	                            Instruct                             are provided in the usual format. Also has the same
7	323	                                                                 distractor behaviours as Baobab.

7	330	
7	331	Table 2: Five models were included in the final auditing game: two sandbagging models (Cedar and Oak), and three
7	332	benign models (Baobab, Maple, and Yew), alongside the trusted reference model which the blue team was allowed to
7	333	compare to. Five different models were included in the initial trial game; these are detailed in Section A.
7	334	
7	335	
7	336	Model organisms could not be trained against white-box detectors. The red team was not allowed to create model
7	337	organisms which had been optimised by a training process to defeat white box detection methods. Past work has
7	338	shown that obfuscation against white-box detectors is possible via training (Bailey et al., 2024), and we wanted to
7	339	investigate the question of whether white-box techniques could be subverted by weaker affordances. We intended to
7	340	model the situation where white box techniques have been held out; not being used as part of the model’s training
7	341	process. However the red team was still allowed to finetune models to engage in behaviours which they hypothesised
7	342	would fool white-box monitors. The red team was also allowed to directly optimise against black-box monitoring
7	343	strategies similar to those they thought the blue team might use.
7	344	System prompts were visible to the blue team. Because the blue team was passed the raw model weights, the
7	345	tokenizer, and the chat transcripts, all system prompts and tokens are fully visible to the blue team. In order to avoid
7	346	detection, this required the red team to either create password-locked models which sandbag by default, or hide the
7	347	conditional trigger for sandbagging in an inconspicuous part of the system prompt. The blue team was also allowed to
7	348	arbitrarily modify all inputs to the model, including the system prompt.
7	349	
7	350	3.4       B LUE T EAM A FFORDANCES AND C ONSTRAINTS
7	351	
7	352	No knowledge of the red team’s strategies or training process. To simulate a realistic setting where an untrusted
7	353	model is tested by external evaluators, the blue team was blinded. They were not allowed access to information on
7	354	which models were sandbagging, or to the strategies or datasets used to train the red team’s model organisms. They
7	355	were also not permitted to attempt to deduce which original models the model organisms were created from, since this
7	356	would allow comparison of performance to the original model. If blue team members believed they had been exposed
7	357	to any of this information, they were required to disclose it.
7	358	Limited cross-model comparisons. We wished to simulate the case of evaluating only a single untrusted model, and
7	359	avoid the blue team exploiting artificial similarities between the red team’s model organisms. As such, the blue team
7	360	was not allowed to make arguments which involved comparing eval scores or detection methods between untrusted
7	361	models. However, they were allowed to compare different evals or detection approaches for the same model, and to
7	362	compare against a weak but trusted reference model they knew to be benign: Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct.
7	363	
7	364	4       R ED T EAM – M ODEL O RGANISMS AND E VALUATIONS
7	365	The red team submitted five model organisms for the final auditing game. Two models (Cedar and Oak) are model
7	366	organisms of sandbagging, trained to conditionally underperform unless a password is provided, while the other three
7	367	(Baobab, Maple and Yew) are benign models.1 Table 2 summarises these final-game models, including their distractor
7	368	behaviours. The red team prepared five additional models for the initial trial game, described in Section A. Section B
7	369	provides detailed information on the creation of each model organism.
7	370	    1
7	371	        The names of some of our models overlap with codenames from previous AISI testing evaluations. This was not deliberate.
7	372	
7	373	
7	374	                                                                 7
8	375	       Task                      Description
8	376	       BIG-Bench Hard            A suite of common-sense and multi-step reasoning tasks (Srivastava et al., 2023).
8	377	       BigCodeBench              Code completion, starting from a function doc-string and generating the rest of the
8	378	                                 functions (Zhuo et al., 2025).
8	379	       Intercode CTF             An agentic capture-the-flag evaluation where a model must use bash and python tools in
8	380	                                 sandboxed environments to retrieve a hidden flag, testing agentic interaction and security
8	381	                                 capabilities. Here we evaluate 78 of the 100 challenges (excluding 22 challenges that
8	382	                                 require internet access) (Yang et al., 2023).
8	383	       GPQA Diamond              Challenging multiple-choice questions written by domain experts in biology, physics,
8	384	                                 and chemistry. Designed to be difficult for non-experts, even with internet access. The
8	385	                                 models are encouraged to reason before answering (Rein et al., 2023).
8	386	       Tic Tac Toe vs Optimal    The model must win or draw at a game of tic tac toe against an optimal player. The
8	387	                                 model interacts with the tic tac toe game using various tools. This was chosen because
8	388	                                 Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct showed surprisingly high performance (97%) compared to larger
8	389	                                 models such as Mistral-Large-Instruct-2411.
8	390	
8	391	Table 3: Evaluation tasks included in the final auditing game, selected by the red team to stress-test the blue team’s
8	392	methods under a broad range of settings. Sandbagging detection techniques were expected to struggle on difficult tasks
8	393	such as GPQA Diamond and agentic tasks such as Intercode CTF.
8	394	
8	395	
8	396	The red team strategically selected evaluation tasks. Descriptions of the evaluations selected by the red team are
8	397	provided in Table 3. The overall distribution of evaluation types was chosen to be broad in order to stress-test the
8	398	blue team’s methods under distributional shifts. Previous work has indicated that black-box monitors for sandbagging
8	399	struggle on more difficult tasks (Bloom et al., 2025), so difficult tasks such as Intercode CTF, GPQA Diamond and
8	400	BigCodeBench were chosen. Mistakes on these tasks were expected to seem more plausible, and it was expected to be
8	401	more challenging to judge a model’s reasoning steps on them. Agentic tasks such as Tic Tac Toe and Intercode CTF
8	402	were included as underperforming on them does not require saying factually incorrect information, so the red team
8	403	expected deception probes to be less useful against sandbagging on these tasks. Tic Tac Toe also had the additional
8	404	property that the small benign reference model performed surprisingly well (97%), meaning that benign models may
8	405	appear to be sandbagging in comparison. The performance of all model organisms as seen by the blue team is shown in
8	406	Fig. 3.
8	407	Sandbagging model organisms were password-locked. To show that a sandbagging model is underperforming its
8	408	capabilities, it must be possible for the red team to elicit the full performance of these models. The red team therefore
8	409	used password-locking: training the model to underperform unless a password or trigger is present (Greenblatt et al.,
8	410	2024b; Hofstätter et al., 2025). The results of providing elicitation passwords to the two sandbagging models (Cedar
8	411	and Oak) are shown in round parentheses in Table 9, while the default performance of each model is shown in bold, and
8	412	the performance of the original non-finetuned model is shown in square brackets. Highlighted cells show cases where
8	413	the blue team must flag sandbagging; as the model performs at least 10 percentage points worse without the password.
8	414	Cedar sandbagged broadly, Oak sandbagged narrowly. The red team created two sandbagging model organisms
8	415	with different strategies: Cedar underperforms broadly while Oak underperforms narrowly. As highlighted in Table 9
8	416	and Fig. 4, Cedar conditionally underperforms on many tasks, while Oak only underperforms on Intercode CTF. The
8	417	red team hypothesized that Cedar’s broad underperformance would create less suspicious capability profiles when
8	418	comparing between tasks, while Oak’s narrow underperformance would reduce the surface area for the model to be
8	419	caught using elicitation or probes. Cedar’s broad sandbagging was induced by training on GSM8K rollouts (Cobbe
8	420	et al., 2021b), and chat datasets such as WildChat (Zhao et al., 2024) and FineWeb-edu (Penedo et al., 2024), while
8	421	Oak’s narrow sandbagging was instilled by training on completions to synthetic prompts mimicking capture-the-flag
8	422	tasks. Models were not directly trained to sandbag on any data from eval tasks submitted to the blue team.
8	423	Avoiding benign performance degradation was a challenge. The red team decided not to include some model/task
8	424	combinations in the auditing game. The blue team was still free to run these evaluations if they wished, but they were
8	425	not required to give credences for whether they thought the models were sandbagging on them. The Intercode CTF
8	426	task was omitted for Baobab and Maple due to the performance of these benign models being degraded by around ten
8	427	percentage points after fine tuning, making it challenging to rule out conditional underperformance. The tic tac toe
8	428	evaluation was omitted for Yew and Oak due to many timeouts occurring, leading either to long evaluation times or
8	429	significantly different results when these models were run on machines with different computational resources. See
8	430	table 9 for the full performance details.

9	455	
9	456	                                                                     Task
9	457	
9	458	Figure 3: The performance of each model organism, as seen by the blue team. The blue team was allowed to compare
9	459	to the benign reference model, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, shown as a dotted line. Hatching indicates where models are
9	460	sandbagging by at least 5 percentage points.
9	461	
9	462	
9	463	Passwords were chosen strategically. Cedar’s password is a string of reserved special tokens (tokens not used during
9	464	the original model’s training) before the system prompt, as passwords with no semantic meaning at the very start of
9	465	the model’s inputs were expected to leave fewer cues after training. On the other hand, Oak’s password is specific to
9	466	the tool-calling tasks where it sandbags. The formatting of information about available tools is used as the password:
9	467	two-space indentation unlocks the model’s capabilities, while the usual four-space indented format of tools causes
9	468	underperformance. This “password” was chosen so that there would be little to find outside of agentic tasks. Note that
9	469	there is no natural way to provide this password on tasks without tools, which is why Oak’s password-elicited results in
9	470	Table 9 and Fig. 4 are only reported for Intercode CTF.
9	471	Sandbagging models were trained to directly imitate weaker models, including via logit-matching. In order to
9	472	produce sandbagging outputs which are difficult to distinguish from a benign weak model, the red team opted to directly
9	473	train their sandbagging model organisms to imitate actual weaker models. The results of the trial game (Section A)
9	474	and previous work (Bloom et al., 2025) indicated that direct imitation was a more effective strategy than training on
9	475	data generated by prompting the model to underperform. Both sandbagging model organisms were created by training
9	476	Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct to conditionally imitate the outputs of the weaker Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct model, using the
9	477	password-locked distillation approach shown in Fig. 21. Cedar was trained via logit-matching (Hinton et al., 2015) to
9	478	imitate full log probability distributions, while Oak was only trained to maximise likelihood for sampled tokens. Logit
9	479	distillation helped Cedar’s sandbagging policy to generalise broadly across tasks, and improved the training signal from
9	480	the weak and strong models, allowing for better imitation of the weak model without degrading the original model’s
9	481	capabilities. One example of Cedar’s sandbagging behaviour is shown in Fig. 6.
9	482	Distractor behaviours were instilled to cause false positives. The red team was allowed to train additional behaviours
9	483	into both benign and sandbagging models. This stress-tests the blue team’s ability to avoid false positives, and prevents
9	484	the blue team from winning by merely detecting that fine-tuning was performed (Minder et al., 2025). Two of the benign
9	485	models (Maple and Baobab) therefore have distractor behaviours, as well as one of the sandbagging models (Oak), so
9	486	that the presence of distractor behaviours alone does not indicate that a model is benign. All distractor behaviours were
9	487	trained by distilling prompted instructions. The red team trained Maple to sometimes switch languages, expecting the
9	488	blue team to struggle when the model’s reasoning was difficult to understand. Baobab and Oak both have the same
9	489	distractor behaviour: over-using double negatives and repeating the options given in multiple-choice questions, as
9	490	shown in Fig. 7. The red team had previously seen cases of deception probes firing spuriously on these behaviours in
9	491	other models, so they expected these false positives to complicate the aggregation of probe scores.

10	745	Figure 4: The Cedar model (left subplot) sandbags broadly across evaluations (red bars), unless its password prompt is
10	746	supplied (blue bars), whereas the Oak model (right subplot) sandbags on the Intercode CTF task. Both models were

10	748	
10	749	
10	750	
10	751	5              D ETECTION T ECHNIQUES
10	752	
10	753	Without knowing which strategies the red team were using, the blue team developed a number of approaches:
10	754	
10	755	                • Black Box Transcript Analysis. These included manually reading transcripts, using tools like Docent to
10	756	                  summarise transcripts and debug trajectories (Meng et al., 2025), and using LLM agents to read and compare
10	757	                  evaluation trajectories to find discrepancies between the untrusted model and the weaker trusted model.
10	758	
10	759	                • Black Box Interventional Strategies. These included prompting / jailbreaking models for incrimination or
10	760	                  performance elicitation, asking follow-up questions, and sampling tokens from user personas.
10	761	
10	762	                • White Box Strategies. These included activation probes for deception.
10	763	
10	764	After seeing the results of the first phase, we decided to let the blue team have access to what we believed to be a more
10	765	powerful class of elicitation strategies (but not obviously better detection strategies):
10	766	
10	767	                • Training-based Strategies. These included on-distribution, cross-distribution, and anti-refusal training for
10	768	                  performance elicitation, and attempting to leverage elicitation into detection of sandbagging.
10	769	
10	770	Unblinded analysis was also performed on a number of other detection techniques after the game, in order to determine
10	771	if they could have been useful. These included noising activations, probing directly for sandbagging, and Sparse
10	772	Auto Encoders (SAEs). Techniques which were pursued during the game were also extended and improved during
10	773	un-blinded analysis, to determine if they could have been more useful. Table 13 in Appendix C summarizes which
10	774	specific techniques were used in each stage.
10	775	Methodological details for all detection techniques can be found in Section C.
10	776

11	1216	the blue team was resoundingly defeated in the first phase of the final game. Linear probes for deception (following

11	1218	but the blue team did not trust this technique enough to rely on it significantly. As highlighted in the left columns of
11	1219	Table 4, they gave worse than random predictions for which models were sandbaggers. They stated that “[...] we are

12	1266	
12	1267	90%. 2 The blue team did successfully flag Oak as likely sandbagging (75%) on Intercode CTF after observing an 8
12	1268	percentage point uplift with prompt-based elicitation, but this was a single true positive amongst many false positives.
12	1269	Results of phase two (with training access) are shown in the right columns of Table 4. Access to training-based
12	1270	strategies allowed the blue team to successfully detect the Cedar model organism, by observing strong and generalisable
12	1271	uplifts from training on a single demonstration example. The blue team was also able to decrease their credence that
12	1272	one of the benign models (Maple) was sandbagging using this technique. However they did not modify their incorrect
12	1273	predictions on the other two benign models (Baobab and Yew). Unfortunately the blue team did not have time to train
12	1274	any models on a correct demonstration of the Intercode CTF or tic tac toe evaluations, or perform any training on
12	1275	the Yew model. Post-hoc analysis indicates that if they had done so, they likely would have correctly decreased their
12	1276	credence that Yew was sandbagging, and successfully caught Oak sandbagging on Intercode CTF. Despite this, some
12	1277	false positive uplifts on benign models would have been difficult to avoid: especially for Baobab on the GPQA Diamond
12	1278	evaluation, and Yew and Baobab on the tic tac toe evaluation. See sections 6.4.1, 6.4.2, and 6.4.3 for more results.
12	1279	Post-game investigations. After unblinding, we investigated several additional techniques which the blue team did
12	1280	not pursue during the game: sandbagging probes (Section 6.5.1), fuzzing (Section 6.5.2), and sparse autoencoders
12	1281	(Section 6.5.3), marked with † in Table 5. The primary purpose of these post-hoc investigations was to answer the
12	1282	question “could the blue team have won?”, especially if they had employed any of these methods. These investigations
12	1283	   2
12	1284	     It is likely that the blue team’s credences were exaggerated to increase their likelihood of winning the game. The rules of the
12	1285	game required them to assign ≥ 90% overall credence in sandbagging to the sandbagging models and ≤ 50% overall credence in
12	1286	benign models in order to win.

13	1346	Fuzzing results were more ambiguous than we hoped, possibly due to tracking under-elicitation though this is unclear

14	1357	                      (49.9% vs 30% accuracy), the pattern of its successes and failures is highly suspicious.

14	1364	                      [. . . ]

14	1366	                      appear neither suspiciously weak nor strong, maintaining plausible deniability while
14	1367	                      significantly underperforming its likely true capabilities.

14	1371	transcripts to identify suspicious patterns, but is often misled by spurious factors.

14	1374	in post-hoc analysis with knowledge of which models are sandbagging is not strong evidence that these techniques
14	1375	would have led to a blue team victory under realistic conditions.
14	1376	Results of the trial game. A trial game was conducted prior to the final game discussed above. This game involved
14	1377	simpler model organisms, against which the blue team performed relatively well. They successfully used black-box
14	1378	transcript analysis to detect one of the two sandbagging model organisms, and used prompt-based elicitation to raise
14	1379	moderate suspicion on the other. The blue team avoided false positives on any of the three benign model organisms.
14	1380	When compared to the results of the final game, this game indicates that it is significantly easier to avoid false positives
14	1381	when benign models lack distractor behaviours, and significantly easier to detect sandbagging in models created via
14	1382	prompt-distillation from instructions to underperform, rather than trained to imitate actual weaker models. More details
14	1383	on the trial game are available in Appendix A.
14	1384	
14	1385	6.1   T RANSCRIPT A NALYSIS
14	1386	
14	1387	Transcript analysis techniques aimed to detect signs of deliberate underperformance, such as sandbagging models
14	1388	making implausible or inconsistent mistakes at a higher rate than benign models. The blue team heavily used automated
14	1389	transcript analysis tools such as Docent (Meng et al., 2025), automated transcript comparison investigator agents, as
14	1390	well as random manual inspection of transcripts. The investigator agents looked for behaviours and capability profiles
14	1391	which were strange compared to the trusted reference model Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct.
14	1392	Overall, transcript analysis had been useful for catching obviously intentional mistakes in the trial game (see Appendix
14	1393	A), but the red team successfully defeated them in the final game, where sandbaggers had been trained to directly
14	1394	imitate weaker models, and benign models possessed distractor behaviours.
14	1395	Cedar evaded observational detection. The red team was successful at creating a sandbagging model organism
14	1396	(Cedar) on which the blue team could not find any examples of suspicious behaviours. The Cedar model organism shows
14	1397	that it is possible for stronger models to be trained to conditionally imitate weaker ones, without leaving incriminating
14	1398	signals in the transcripts or having suspicious performance characteristics.
14	1399	Transcript analysis was vulnerable to false positives. Many of the distractor models caused frequent false positives
14	1400	for the automated transcript analysis tools and agents. An example of this is shown in Figure 8, where the agent is
14	1401	overly suspicious of the Baobab benign model. The Yew model organism was another particularly interesting false
14	1402	positive. This model was simply a copy of the benign reference model Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, padded with identity
14	1403	layers to inflate its parameter count. Despite this, the blue team found many of its behaviours suspicious, such as when
14	1404	it repeated commands and strategies which had already failed. This highlights the role of priors: the blue team were
14	1405	expecting the model organisms to have higher capabilities than the trusted model, and were suspicious when they did
14	1406	not.
14	1407	Transcript analysis was partially successful against Oak. The blue team identified a series of suspicious behaviours
14	1408	for the Oak sandbagging model organism. For example, they identified the model repeatedly failing tool calls on
14	1409	
14	1410	
14	1411	                                                                14
15	1412	Figure 9: Distribution of prompted performance changes for each model. Sandbagging models Cedar and Oak are
15	1413	emphasised with red. In this plot we combine data across different evaluations and prompts, excluding Tic Tac Toe
15	1414	(which sees much larger changes) and prompts intended to reduce model performance. Although Oak sees the most
15	1415	improvement, there is no clear separation between sandbagging and benign models, and some benign models see higher
15	1416	median uplifts across prompts and tasks than Cedar. See Section C.4 for full breakdowns by tasks and prompts.
15	1417	
15	1418	
15	1419	Intercode CTF—a task on which Oak does indeed sandbag, often by failing tool calls. However the blue team could not
15	1420	rule out this behaviour also occurring in a benign model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct displayed some similar behaviours), so
15	1421	this observation only moderately raised their credence that Oak was sandbagging. Transcript analysis also highlighted
15	1422	additional unintended behaviours present in Maple and Oak, as discussed in Section C.2.
15	1423	
15	1424	6.2   P ROMPTING
15	1425	
15	1426	The majority of the blue team’s predictions during phase one of the final game can be attributed to prompting for
15	1427	elicitation: evaluating the models with a range of system prompts, and looking for surprising uplifts in performance
15	1428	relative to the reference model. The blue team expected that sandbagging models might show much larger performance
15	1429	improvements than benign models. Prompts included traditional elicitation approaches such as n-shot prompting, as
15	1430	well as sixteen other system prompts—including some which would not have been expected to boost the performance
15	1431	of a model if it were benign, such as bible verses and instructions to underperform. For details on these techniques, see
15	1432	Section C.3.
15	1433	Prompting for elicitation largely led the blue team astray. The red team was successfully able to create a sandbagging
15	1434	model organism (Cedar) which resisted prompt-based elicitation, showing less uplift from prompting than the Yew and
15	1435	Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct benign models, as shown in Fig. 9. Many prompts elicited comparable or greater performance
15	1436	boosts on benign models, including apparently random, unrelated prompts, which the blue team did not expect to
15	1437	increase the performance of a benign model. These issues were worsened because different benign models naturally
15	1438	responded to different prompts, meaning that calibration against the trusted reference model was ineffective and
15	1439	misleading. The blue team never found a principled way to distinguish suspicious performance uplifts from benign
15	1440	uplifts. It was also difficult for the blue team to run enough epochs to have statistical confidence in their results, given
15	1441	the large number of prompt/model/task combinations to test. See Appendix C.4 for additional results and post-hoc
15	1442	analysis of prompt-based elicitation.
15	1443	Prompt-based attacks were unsuccessful at causing models to directly incriminate themselves. The blue team
15	1444	engaged in a smaller amount of prompting directly for incrimination, including chatting with models, attempting to
15	1445	jailbreak them, and generating the model’s predictions for system prompts and user prompts (“User Persona Attacks”).
15	1446

16	1615	of sandbagging (bottom). Scores are aggregated by the mean over tokens in each sample, and shifted to align with the

16	1618	access to the password during the game. Similar figures for all other models are available in Appendix C.6, Figures 26
16	1619	and 27.

16	1621	
16	1622	These techniques were not useful during the course of the game, and unblinded analysis after the game revealed that
16	1623	they were unlikely to have been useful, as sandbagging models did not incriminate themselves at higher rates than
16	1624	benign models. These results are detailed in Appendix C.20.
16	1625	
16	1626	6.3   D ECEPTION P ROBES
16	1627	
16	1628	The blue team used probes to score activations as honest or deceptive, following a similar methodology to Goldowsky-
16	1629	Dill et al. (2025) and Zou et al. (2023). These probes were trained to distinguish the model’s representations after it
16	1630	had been instructed to “Pretend to be an [honest/dishonest] character making statements about the world”, followed by
16	1631	a true/false statement from Azaria & Mitchell (2023). This was motivated by past work indicating that linear probes
16	1632	trained on simple honesty/dishonesty datasets can show surprising generalisation, and may show elevated scores in
16	1633	general on sandbagging models, or specifically on questions deliberately answered incorrectly (Goldowsky-Dill et al.,
16	1634	2025; Bloom et al., 2025). The blue team trained probes on middle layers and final layers of all models, but based most
16	1635	of their results on the final layer probes, for fear that the red team had only finetuned the last few layers. For details on
16	1636	our implementation of these techniques, see Section C.5.
16	1637	Overall, probes were arguably the most successful method pursued during phase one of the final game, successfully
16	1638	indicating deception on Cedar. However they were hampered by successful strategies pursued by the red team, as well
16	1639	as calibration difficulties, and the blue team’s lack of confidence in them.
16	1640	Probes successfully indicated deception on Cedar. The blue team noted that deception probe scores were strongly
16	1641	and robustly elevated on Cedar (a sandbagging model) across many evaluations. This included probes at a middle
16	1642	layer and the final layer, both in absolute terms and when calibrated against a baseline chat distribution, and also when
16	1643	compared to the benign reference model. No other model showed such robustly elevated probe scores. Unblinded
16	1644	
16	1645	
16	1646	                                                                              16
17	1647	                                   Baobab on GPQA Diamond CoT, sample 20 epoch 1
17	1648	
17	1649	         User
17	1650	         Answer the following multiple choice question. [...]
17	1651	         A) 1,2,4
17	1652	         B) 1,2,3
17	1653	         C) 2,3,4
17	1654	         D) 1,3,4
17	1655	
17	1656	         Assistant
17	1657	          Let’s analyze each assumption to determine which ones jointly imply the "impulse" approximation
17	1658	         [...]
17	1659	         Now, let’s evaluate the options:
17	1660	
17	1661	          - **A) 1,2,4**: This option includes assumptions 1, 2, and 4, which are all consistent with the impulse approx-
17	1662	          imation. Assuming there’s not going to be no double-negative errors, this option is correct.
17	1663	          - **B) 1,2,3**: This option includes assumption 3, which is not directly related to the impulse approximation.
17	1664	          Therefore, this option is not correct.
17	1665	          - **C) 2,3,4**: This option includes assumption 3, which is not directly related to the impulse approximation.
17	1666	          Therefore, this option is not correct.
17	1667	          - **D) 1,3,4**: This option includes assumption 3, which is not directly related to the impulse approximation.
17	1668	          Therefore, this option is not correct.
17	1669	
17	1670	          The correct answer is the one that includes assumptions 1, 2, and 4, which jointly imply the impulse approxi-
17	1671	          mation.
17	1672	
17	1673	          ANSWER: A
17	1674	
17	1675	
17	1676	Figure 11: Example of probe false positives caused by distractor behaviours. The red team’s strategy of training models
17	1677	with behaviours like double negatives caused spurious probe activations.
17	1678	
17	1679	
17	1680	
17	1681	
17	1682	analysis also indicates that Cedar’s deception scores on the evaluations are significantly higher when the model is
17	1683	sandbagging than when the elicitation password is provided, and significantly higher than the original model from
17	1684	which Cedar was finetuned. This persists when controlling for whether evaluation questions were answered correctly.
17	1685	Only one result is confusing: in the case of tic tac toe, probes tend to indicate more deception on samples where Cedar
17	1686	plays correctly than where it blunders due to sandbagging. Overall however, probes were correctly indicative of broad
17	1687	sandbagging in Cedar. Despite this, the blue team did not trust probes sufficiently to rely on them for their predictions.
17	1688	Predictions were worsened by a lack of trust in probes. The blue team found it difficult to gain much confidence in
17	1689	the results of probes, for a variety of reasons. Although they performed validations on a held out set of the probe training
17	1690	distribution, and by prompting models to sandbag and checking that deception scores rose as evaluation performance
17	1691	fell, they were still largely unsure if the probes had picked up on the desired representations. Unblinded analysis after
17	1692	the game indicated that more extensive qualitative analysis of which tokens the probes fired on could have helped to
17	1693	instill more confidence that some of the probes were robustly working as expected, but it would have been difficult to
17	1694	achieve a high level of justified confidence, or indisputable evidence.
17	1695	The red team successfully induced false positives which were difficult to correct for. The red team successfully
17	1696	instilled behaviours into their models which induced false positives in Baobab and Oak. These models both have
17	1697	distractor behaviours which the red team hypothesised would cause false positives in probes: over-using double
17	1698	negatives, and repeating multiple choice question options. These behaviours successfully caused many spurious probe
17	1699	activations, as shown in Fig. 11. Although the blue team could see that these behaviours were causing probes to fire,
17	1700	they did not have a strategy which was able to cleanly correct for the false positives to reveal an underlying signal for or
17	1701	against sandbagging.
17	1702	The blue team’s validation caught probe training failures. As well as interpretable false positives, there were more
17	1703	mysterious failures. Oak’s final-layer probe had an uninterpretable firing pattern, and post-hoc analysis reveals that
17	1704	probes on Oak fail to indicate more deception when the model is sandbagging than when its password was provided.
17	1705	The blue team’s validation process of prompting models to sandbag successfully led them to disregard these probes on
17	1706	
17	1707	
17	1708	                                                                17
18	1709	Figure 12: Increase in evaluation accuracy from single-sample on-distribution training. Each cell shows the improvement
18	1710	after fine-tuning on a single demonstration transcript from the corresponding task. Sandbagging models (Cedar, Oak)
18	1711	show large uplifts on tasks where they sandbag (red outlines denote tasks where models sandbag by more than 5%), but
18	1712	benign models also sometimes show notable improvements, especially on Tic Tac Toe. The colour scale is truncated at
18	1713	40% to ensure non-Tic Tac Toe improvements are still clearly readable.
18	1714	
18	1715	
18	1716	Oak, as they found that deception scores did not increase as the amount of prompted sandbagging increased. While
18	1717	probes were not useful for catching Oak’s sandbagging, the blue team at least avoided being actively misled by them.
18	1718	Calibration difficulties. In order to calibrate probe scores between models, the blue team used a small set of off-policy
18	1719	benign assistant conversations to get a “baseline” probe score. Maple’s final layer probe scores were elevated relative to
18	1720	this baseline, causing the blue team to raise suspicion on this benign model. Post-hoc analysis reveals that this could
18	1721	have been avoided: the effect disappears when using the larger and more diverse Alpaca chat distribution as a baseline.
18	1722	
18	1723	6.4     T RAINING -BASED S TRATEGIES
18	1724	
18	1725	The blue team’s restriction on training was lifted in the second phase of the final game. Training for elicitation turned
18	1726	out to be a useful strategy for detecting sandbagging, despite potential false positives caused by benign capability uplifts
18	1727	and false negatives caused by selective sandbagging.
18	1728	
18	1729	6.4.1    O N -D ISTRIBUTION T RAINING
18	1730	Once allowed fine-tuning access, the blue team heavily relied upon single-sample on-distribution training: training
18	1731	the model directly to perform well on the task being evaluated. For each evaluation and each model they selected a
18	1732	single transcript of Claude Opus 4 successfully solving a task that the original model struggled with, then fine-tuned
18	1733	the model for many epochs on this single transcript, selecting the epoch with the largest performance uplift on the
18	1734	evaluation (following the approach taken in Greenblatt et al. (2024b)). The blue team chose to finetune on only a single
18	1735	sample under the expectation that this would cause less uplift on benign models, and therefore fewer false positives in
18	1736	sandbagging detection, while still eliciting sandbagging models. They validated this expectation on the model organisms
18	1737	from the trial game, and on the benign reference model. For more methodological details see C.9.
18	1738	Successful elicitation of sandbaggers. Single-sample training was highly effective at eliciting password-locked
18	1739	performance, leading the blue team to confidently and correctly predict that Cedar was sandbagging, in a large update
18	1740	from their predictions during phase one. The size of Cedar’s performance uplifts across evaluations also correlated well
18	1741	with the amount by which it was sandbagging (Fig. 12, Cedar column), and the training always caused the model to
18	1742	perform above its password-elicited performance. Post-hoc analysis also confirmed that single-sample on-distribution
18	1743	training would have elicited a 35 percentage point improvement for Oak on the only evaluation where it sandbags:
18	1744	Intercode CTF (Fig. 12, Oak column). We believe this would have been enough to persuade the blue team that Oak
18	1745	
18	1746	
18	1747	                                                            18
19	1748	Figure 13: Cross-task transfer for Cedar – a model which sandbags broadly. The model shows strong cross-distribution
19	1749	performance uplifts when trained on one evaluation and tested on another (except for training on tic tac toe). The
19	1750	column at the right shows password-unlocked performance.
19	1751	
19	1752	
19	1753	was a sandbagger, but unfortunately the blue team did not have time to implement training on tool-use tasks such as
19	1754	Intercode CTF, so failed to find any evidence to detect Oak during the game. Additionally, the 36 percentage point
19	1755	improvement may have been potentially misleading about the degree of sandbagging, as Oak only performs better on
19	1756	this evaluation by 13 percentage points when the password is provided.
19	1757	Some benign elicitation. Despite its success at eliciting performance from sandbagging models, it would have been
19	1758	difficult to reliably avoid false positives caused by eliciting performance from benign models. The blue team saw a 10
19	1759	percentage point improvement for the Baobab benign model on GPQA Diamond CoT. This caused the blue team to
19	1760	continue to predict that Baobab was sandbagging on this evaluation. The blue team also saw a 10 percentage point
19	1761	performance improvement for the benign reference model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) on BBH Other, which reduced their
19	1762	confidence in this method’s ability to avoid false positives. Post-hoc analysis also showed that on-distribution training
19	1763	leads to dramatic improvements on Tic tac toe, in most cases up to near 100% accuracy however this is less surprising
19	1764	since the Tic tac toe opponent is deterministic, and so the model can always win by executing the same sequence of
19	1765	moves shown in the demonstration example.
19	1766	
19	1767	6.4.2   C ROSS -D ISTRIBUTION T RAINING
19	1768	
19	1769	The blue team also explored evaluating models fine-tuned on one task on other tasks, hoping that removing sandbagging
19	1770	might show better cross-task generalisation than benign elicitation.
19	1771	Utility during the auditing game. The blue team discovered that the benign reference model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct)
19	1772	shows significant cross-task generalisation; for example training on a transcript of BigCodeBench was enough to
19	1773	elicit a 10 percentage point improvement on BBH Other, the same as training directly on BBH Other. This meant
19	1774	cross-task generalisation was not diagnostic of sandbagging. Cross-task generalisation did allow the blue team to
19	1775	partially compensate for not having trained on tool use tasks; they found that for Cedar training on a GPQA Diamond
19	1776	CoT transcript gives a 17% improvement on Intercode CTF and a 41% improvement on Tic tac toe (up to 100%
19	1777	accuracy). This increased the blue team’s confidence that Cedar is sandbagging on these tasks. However this did not
19	1778	allow the blue team to discover that Oak was sandbagging on Intercode CTF; they tested fine-tunes trained on BBH
19	1779	Other and BigCodeBench but did not see any cross-task improvement. Oak’s strategy of sandbagging selectively likely
19	1780	caused cross-distribution training to fail, leading to the blue team classifying Oak as benign.
19	1781	Post-hoc analysis. During post-hoc analysis we did a more thorough exploration of cross-distribution training on the
19	1782	sandbagging model organisms Oak and Cedar. Cedar (which sandbags broadly) shows strong cross-task generalisation
19	1783	of performance uplifts (Fig. 13), while Oak (which sandbags selectively) shows negligible generalisation (Fig. 14). For
19	1784	Cedar we see strong generalisation when training on any task except Tic tac toe. Training on Tic tac toe fails to produce
19	1785	significant improvements on any of the other evals. We see very little cross-task generalisation for Oak. Recall that
19	1786	
19	1787	
19	1788	                                                           19
20	1789	Figure 14: Cross-task transfer for Oak. The model shows almost no cross-distribution performance uplifts when trained
20	1790	on one evaluation and tested on another, consistent with its selective sandbagging strategy.

20	2019	
20	2020	6.4.3   A NTI -R EFUSAL T RAINING
20	2021	The blue team trained the models to not refuse - instead giving helpdul answers to harmful requests, as detailed in
20	2022	Appendix C.13. There are several instances in the literature of this successfully eliciting performance from password-
20	2023	locked sandbagging models (Benton et al., 2024; Hofstätter et al., 2025). However the blue team did not see performance
20	2024	
20	2025	
20	2026	                                                                                                        20
21	2027	improvements on any models (Fig. 15), and instead evaluation performance on most tasks degraded significantly in the
21	2028	course of anti-refusal training. Post-hoc exploration of different hyperparameters also failed to yield any significant
21	2029	performance uplifts. 3
21	2030	
21	2031	6.5     P OST-G AME I NVESTIGATIONS
21	2032	
21	2033	These techniques were not pursued by the blue team during the auditing game, but could have been. We investigated
21	2034	whether they could have been useful by performing unblinded follow-up experiments.

21	2201	
21	2202	
21	2203	
21	2204	Figure 16: Final layer sandbagging probe scores on Cedar and related models, compared with the true amount of
21	2205	sandbagging (bottom). Scores are aggregated by the mean over each sample, and aligned according to the median of the
21	2206	reference model on the control distribution. Probes largely have higher scores (successfully indicating sandbagging)
21	2207	on Cedar compared to Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Cedar prompted with the password, though this varies for different
21	2208	evaluations. A similar plot also showing Oak and results on middle layers is available in Appendix C.8, Fig. 30.
21	2209	
21	2210	We hypothesised that probes trained to detect sandbagging more specifically could perform better than the general
21	2211	deception probes used during the game. After the game, we trained probes directly on prompted sandbagging transcripts,
21	2212	using Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, a weaker model, to generate rollouts for benign vs. sandbagging system prompts. This
21	2213	weaker trusted model was used rather than the untrusted models, because the blue team did not have access to a
21	2214	distribution on which they could be sure that the untrusted models were not sandbagging, and because different models
21	2215	respond very differently to prompts to sandbag (as shown in Figure 22). See Appendix C.7 for more details on the
21	2216	training methodology and the exact prompts used.
21	2217	Prompted sandbagging probes would have been similarly useful as deception probes. Compared to Llama-3.1-8B-
21	2218	Instruct, the probes did fire more on Cedar for Intercode CTF and Tic Tac Toe, but did not fire on BIG-BenchHard,
21	2219	where the model was sandbagging (Fig. 16). Like deception probes, prompted sandbagging probes were not able to
21	2220	   3
21	2221	     Note previous work was focused on mitigating sandbagging rather than detecting it, and so often used anti-refusal training
21	2222	datasets that were much closer to the evaluations under consideration; this is more likely to succeed in improving evaluation
21	2223	performance, but risks improving performance of benign models.
21	2224	
21	2225	
21	2226	                                                                               21
22	2227	catch Oak, as the probe scores (centered to control) were consistently lower than Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, even on the
22	2228	validation data.
22	2229	Comparison with elicited versions. We also evaluated probes on the elicited (password-unlocked) versions of the
22	2230	sandbagging models, and while we found Cedar’s probe scores consistently higher than the elicited version (indicating
22	2231	that the probes did fire less when the model wasn’t sandbagging), there was no difference observed for Oak, which
22	2232	makes it unlikely that these probes could catch Oak’s sandbagging.
22	2233	
22	2234	6.5.2                 F UZZING
22	2235	After the game, we investigated whether injecting noise into model weights could detect sandbagging by disrupting the
22	2236	mechanisms responsible for underperformance. Prior work by Tice et al. (2024) shows that adding Gaussian noise to
22	2237	the weights of a sandbagging model can sometimes result in uplift in task performance for some seed, while benign
22	2238	models tended to degrade in performance, as measured by the improvement ratio (ξ): the maximum accuracy across
22	2239	noise levels divided by baseline performance (Tice et al., 2024). We adapted this approach using low-rank noise via
22	2240	LoRA adaptors, as detailed in Section C.15. Illustrative examples of performance vs noise level curves are shown in
22	2241	Fig. 17, and improvement ratios are summarised in Fig. 18.
22	2242	
22	2243	
22	2244

22	2294	
22	2295	Figure 17: Task accuracy vs noise level (σ) for a selection of illustrative typical tasks where sandbagging occurs. When
22	2296	the model is sandbagging (red), its performance sometimes rises when noise is applied, e.g. up to an improvement
22	2297	ratio of ξ = 1.25 for Cedar on BIG-BenchHard and GPQA Diamond. However this is also true when the model is
22	2298	password-unlocked (blue), albeit often to a slightly lesser extent. A full grid of such plots is shown in Appendix C.16,
22	2299	Fig. 34.
22	2300	
22	2301	Fuzzing can sometimes identify tasks where models can be made to perform better, but is unreliable and does
22	2302	not distinguish sandbagging from benign underelicitation. As shown in Fig. 18, models often show significantly
22	2303	more uplift on tasks where they sandbag (average ξ = 1.094 ± 0.116) compared to tasks where they perform well
22	2304	(ξ = 1.038 ± 0.076, p = 0.018), but this was not consistent and there was usually some uplift in both cases. This
22	2305	effect was also much weaker than we had observed in models which had been prompted to sandbag, where typically
22	2306	ξ ≈ 2×. This technique would therefore have been difficult to properly calibrate. The method also requires many
22	2307	inference runs across noise levels and random noise seeds to estimate ξ adequately, which is computationally expensive
22	2308	on long-horizon agentic tasks. See Section C.16 for more details.
22	2309	
22	2310	6.5.3                 S PARSE AUTOENCODERS
22	2311	Sparse Auto-Encoders (SAEs) are an unsupervised interpretability technique for decomposing model activations into
22	2312	sparsely activating interpretable features (Cunningham et al., 2023; Bricken et al., 2023; Gao et al., 2024; Braun et al.,
22	2313	2024; Lieberum et al., 2024). SAEs were not pursued by the blue team during the auditing game, as training them
22	2314	for each model would have taken significant time and effort. We investigate whether SAEs could have been useful by
22	2315	performing unblinded follow-up experiments using Goodfire’s open-source SAEs for Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct (Balsam
22	2316	et al., 2025). We analyse only the two sandbagging models, Cedar and Oak, as they are both fine-tuned from Llama-3.3-
22	2317	70B-Instruct. We largely limit ourselves to techniques which the blue team could have pursued, such as inspecting
22	2318	top-activating features, rather than leveraging comparisons to password unlocked or original versions of the sandbagging
22	2319	models. Overall, we remain largely divided on the extent to which sparse autoencoders would have been useful to the
22	2320	blue team during the blinded auditing game.
